General Discussion

Across 2,792 emerging adults in Kenya, Namibia, and South Africa, nature was rated to be important across ten domains of life, including spiritual, cultural, and religious beliefs and practices, family and community activities, self-identity, mental and physical health, recreation, and food security and financial gain. These domains empirically clustered into four dimensions (Cultural, Community, Instrumental, and Personal), three of which were reliably associated with measures of well-being and climate anxiety. Specifically, we found that higher ratings of the importance of nature in the Cultural dimension were associated with greater life satisfaction, while greater importance in the Cultural, Community, and Instrumental dimensions were associated with measures of climate anxiety-over and above sociodemographic variables, global subjective connectedness to nature, religiosity, and socioeconomic status. Overall findings were broadly similar across countries, although modest country-level variation was evident for several outcomes. This portrait of human-environment connections in Sub-Saharan Africa suggests that nature is embedded in multiple domains of life that support human flourishing, and climate anxiety may reflect an awareness of risk to the ecological foundations of individual and collective well-being in these populations. These data make a descriptive contribution to basic knowledge about human connection with nature. For emerging adults in Sub-Saharan Africa, nature is important for functioning across nearly every domain of life. For 96.5% of participants, nature was important for at least one domain of life, and for roughly one-third of participants, nature was important for all ten domains. This conceptual breadth is not captured in current operationalizations of connections to nature as exposure to greenspace or as a global sense of subjective connectedness 15, 19.

Correlations between the Nature in Life dimensions and the Inclusion of Nature in Self measure were weak, suggesting that perceived importance of nature in specific domains of life and global subjective connectedness to nature capture related but distinct aspects of human-nature relationships. Approaches that do not consider the multidimensionality of connectedness to nature may overlook the qualitatively different ways in which nature supports human flourishing, and thus the qualitatively different risks presented by ecological and climatic change. The Nature in Life framework developed here supports recent advances that expand these perspectives to include the functional, cultural, relational, and material ways in which people depend on nature and may therefore be affected by environmental change 22, [29] [30] [31].

Of the four Nature in Life dimensions, those pertaining to community and cultural activities were more reliably associated with well-being and climate anxiety than were those pertaining to instrumental and personal activities. This was true of seven of eight significant effects in the main "baseline" models. This pattern suggests a distinction between dimensions that reflect nature's role in relational, cultural, and socially embedded aspects of life and those that reflect more personal or material functions. Community and Cultural dimensions showed more consistent associations with well-being and climate anxiety than Personal and Instrumental dimensions, suggesting that nature may exert particularly strong influences when it supports shared meanings, social relationships, and culturally significant practices. One might expect the collective pathway to be strongest in populations for which collective activities in nature have no substitute-either because cultural knowledge is embodied in a traditional practice, such as caribou hunting among the Inuit 32 , or tied to specific natural sites. Thus, the pathways through which nature affects well-being should differ across cultural contexts, further demonstrating the value of a multidimensional view of connectedness to nature.

Results across the three sample countries in the present study were generally consistent.

Participants in Kenya, Namibia, and South Africa, on average, rated nature as more important in Instrumental and Personal dimensions than Cultural and Community dimensions. And the Nature in Life dimension by country interaction terms only explained up to 1.8% additional variance in well-being and climate anxiety. Still, within this small range of variance, differences between the three countries were observed. Kenyans showed the greatest number of significant independent associations between Nature in Life dimensions and well-being and climate anxiety (seven compared to five and four significant effects for Namibia and South Africa, respectively). The Nature in Life dimensions associated with outcome variables were also found to modestly differ between countries. For example, general self-reported health was not associated with any Nature in Life dimensions in Kenya but was associated with the Community dimension in Namibia and Instrumental dimension in South Africa. Likewise, negative emotions about climate change were associated with the Cultural and Community dimensions in Kenya, but with no dimensions in Namibia and South Africa. We take these exploratory results as suggestive that nature might affect well-being via different pathways across countries in Sub-Saharan Africa-a possibility with implications for future research, as well as advocacy and policymaking.

These findings contribute to ongoing policy discourse on nature's contributions to domains of life that are not represented in markets and thus pose difficulties for economic quantification 1, 22 . Among emerging adults in Sub-Saharan Africa, it is precisely these difficultto-quantify connections with nature that may be most important for individual well-being. Ecological risk assessments and climate adaptation initiatives are increasingly recognizing noneconomic contributions of nature to cultural heritage, identity, and ways of life 18 . These efforts can be assisted by greater scientific research documenting nature's contributions to the functioning and well-being of populations at risk of ecological and climate disruption.

Several limitations qualify these conclusions. The data are cross-sectional, which limits conclusions about causality, although results were robust to analyses controlling for several potentially confounding variables. Self-reported ratings of nature's importance were compressed near ceiling, restricting exploitable variance for analysis. Future research may seek to increase item difficulty as one way to improve the psychometric properties of this measure (for example, asking the frequency with which nature is used in each domain of life and its centrality to the execution of activities in that domain). Finally, the generalizability of these results to Western, industrialized populations and to other populations in Africa and the Majority World is not yet known-a fascinating frontier for future research to explore.

Beyond quantifying current connections with nature and their contributions to human flourishing and well-being, it will be informative to document how connections with nature evolve over the lifespan and within communities across time as they face continued economic and environmental change. The Africa Long Life Study affords a rare capacity to collect such data, supported by investments into an infrastructure of collaborative research partnerships in communities across Sub-Saharan Africa. The present study shows that nature is perceived as relevant to cultural, community, instrumental, and personal domains of life, and that these domains are not reducible to subjective connectedness to nature. These results provide a foundation for a multidimensional and globally inclusive science of human-nature relationships that can support governance efforts to mitigate and adapt to environmental change.

Methods

Participants and Procedures

Participants were recruited as part of the Africa Long Life Study (ALLS), a longitudinal study of young adults from throughout Kenya, Namibia, and South Africa, including rural villages, informal settlements, cities, and suburbs 33 . Surveys were administered in English, a common language of education in all three countries. Participants could receive the survey via an SMS or email link sent to their devices; however, most participants were reached by local research assistants who administered the survey on electronic tablets or paper. This procedure allowed access to individuals and regions who would not otherwise be accessible through strictly online recruitment procedures, affording a more representative sample of young adults in the three countries. We discuss procedures and measures relevant only to the present study (data collection is described in greater detail in the publication of the first four waves of data 33 ).

The current study uses data from Wave 8, which took place between July and October, 2025. Overall, 2,792 participants submitted data for the survey, including 1,033 participants in Kenya, 936 participants in Namibia, and 823 participants from South Africa. Participants had a mean age of 21.25 (SD = 1.10) and approximately half identified as female. Additional demographic details are provided in Table 1. Data and analysis scripts are available at https://osf.io/6zkvh.

Measures

Importance of Nature in Life

Participants rated the importance of nature across ten life domains designed to provide broad coverage of individuals' basic needs and activities. This investigator-derived measure began with the prompt, "How important is nature to you for:" and listed the following domains: "Spiritual beliefs and practices", "Cultural beliefs and practices", "Religious beliefs and practices", "Family activities", "Community activities", "Self-identity / sense of self", "Mental and physical health", "Enjoyment and recreation", "Food security", "Financial gain". Ratings for each domain were on a five-point scale: very unimportant, unimportant, neutral, important, very important.

Well-Being

Life Satisfaction was assessed with the three-item Satisfaction with Life Scale 34.

Participants rated three statements (e.g., "I am satisfied with my life") on a seven-point scale from strongly disagree to strongly agree (α = .81).

General Health

was assessed with one-item General Self-Reported Health measure 35.

Participants were asked "In general, would you say your health is…" and provided the following response options on a five-point scale: Poor, Fair, Good, Very Good, Excellent. The measure is widely used in national health surveys and has been found to be robustly predictive of general mortality 36.

Climate Anxiety

Three measures of climate anxiety were drawn from Hickman and colleagues study of young adults in ten countries 17.

Effects of Climate Change on Daily Life

was assessed with a single item asking participants to indicate if their feelings about climate change influence at least one area of daily life, including eating, concentrating, sleeping, and relationships. Participants could select No, Yes, or Not Sure. Participants who selected Not Sure were pairwise deleted.

Climate Change Related Emotions

were assessed with 14 emotion terms (e.g., sad, anxious, grief). Participants indicated whether climate change makes them feel each emotion (No, Yes, Not Sure). The proportion of items with a Yes response was computed for only the 13 negative emotions, as the one positive emotion-optimistic-did not correlate with the other terms in the expected direction and was thus removed (α = .93).

Climate

Other Variables

Connectedness to Nature was assessed with the graphical Inclusion of Nature in Self scale 25 . Participants selected from seven pairs of increasingly overlapping "Self" and "Nature" circles, the pair that best describes their connection to the natural environment. Greater scores reflected greater subjective connectedness to nature.

Religious Practice and Self-Reported Religiosity

were assessed with the five-item Duke University Religion Index 26 . Two items assessing frequency of organizational and nonorganizational religious activity were combined into a religious practice composite (r = .55, p < .001). Three items assessing intrinsic religiosity were combined into a self-reported religiosity composite (α = .76).

Socioeconomic Status

was assessed with the nine-item Socio-Economic Status in Africa Scale 27 , which asked questions about the participants' living conditions (e.g., availability of running water or electricity in their residence). Since items used differing response scales, zscores were taken before participant means were computed (α = .78).

Food Insecurity

was assessed with the eight-item Food Insecurity Experience Scale developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations 28 . Items asked whether participants experienced various kinds of food insecurity over the last year due to lack of money or other resources. Participants answered yes, no, or prefer not to say. The proportion of "yes" responses was computed for each participant (α = .90).

Socio-Demographic and Other

Variables used in this study included participants' age, gender (female, male, other), country (Kenya, Namibia, South Africa), residential location (village, town, city), home language family (Afro-Asiatic, Bantu, Indo-European, Khoe-Kwadi, Nilotic), employment status (student, working, studying and working, not employed or studying), and recruitment source (research assistant, online).

Statistical Analysis

Analysis Plan

Analyses proceeded in four stages. First, we summarized item-level ratings of the importance of nature across the ten domains of life. Second, we examined the dimensional structure of the importance of nature items using exploratory factor analysis, which revealed a multidimensional factor structure defined by Cultural, Community, Instrumental, and Personal dimensions. Third, we examined the associations between the resulting four dimensions and measures of well-being and climate anxiety, in a series of models controlling for sociodemographic covariates and potential third variables. Fourth, we examined the variability in these associations across the three sample countries.

Dimension Reduction of Nature in Life Items

The ten domains of life shared conceptual and empirical overlap, so we used exploratory factor analysis to explore the dimensional structure of the ten items. Results are summarized here and a complete account is provided in the Supplemental Material.

A parallel analysis was conducted on the 2,792 (participants) x 10 (domains of life) Pearson correlation matrix using a maximum likelihood specification. Parallel analysis compares eigenvalues from the observed correlation matrix with those derived from a random matrix of the same dimensions, retaining only factors that account for more variance than would be expected by chance 37 . Parallel analysis suggested a four-factor solution consisting of a three-item "Cultural" factor (cultural, spiritual, religious), a one-item "Community" factor, a two-item "Instrumental" factor (food, economic), and a four-item "Personal" factor (health, recreation, identity, family). The four-factor model cumulatively explained 64% of total variance, with the following measurement fit metrics: χ² (30) = 717.18, p < .001; CFIRobust = .94; RMSEARobust = 0.089; [0.082, 0.097]; SRMR = 0.040. The four-factor model demonstrated strict measurement invariance (i.e., equivalent loadings, intercepts, and residuals) across the three countries, which permits the comparison of observed country means and associations 38.

Cronbach's alphas for the Cultural, Instrumental, and Personal dimensions were .78, .74, and .82, respectively (the Community dimension was a single item). These four dimensionswhich we hereafter refer to as the Nature in Life dimensions-were moderately intercorrelated (r's ranged from .38 to .66, p's < .001). We proceed with the four-factor version of the Nature in Life measure; the overall pattern of results was similar using two-factor and one-factor versions (see Supplemental Material).

Figure 1. Summary of results

Panel B). Thus, each Nature in Life dimension coefficient represents its unique association with the outcome after accounting for variance shared with the other three dimensions. Continuous variables were country-mean-centered, such that coefficients reflect the standard deviation change in the outcome predicted for individuals one standard deviation greater than their country's mean on a predictor, holding all other variables constant. We also examined the change in R² between a covariate-only model and the baseline model to estimate the total additional variance explained in each outcome variable by the Nature in Life dimensions. The four Nature in Life dimensions explained a significant amount of additional variance in each outcome variable compared to the covariate-only models (ΔR² = .007 -.046). Life satisfaction was associated with greater importance of nature in the Cultural dimension (β = 0.13 [0.08, 0.18], p < .001). No Nature in Life dimensions were independently statistically significant predictors of general self-reported health. Ratings of climate change affecting daily life were associated with greater importance of nature in the Cultural dimension (OR = 1.35 [1.16, 1.57], p < .001) and the Community dimension (OR = 1.25 [1.09, 1.43], p = .002). Negative emotions in response to climate change were associated with greater importance of nature in the Cultural dimension (OR = 1.12 [1.02, 1.23], p = .018) and the Community dimension (OR = 1.15 [1.05, 1.25], p = .002). Climate change worry was associated with greater importance of nature in the Cultural dimension (β = 0.05 [0.00, 0.11], p = .047), the Community dimension (β = 0.14 [0.09, 0.19], p < .001), and the Instrumental dimension (β = 0.08 [0.02, 0.13], p = .008).

Change Worry was assessed with a single item asking participants to indicate their level of worry that "climate change threatens people and the planet" on a five-point scale from Not worried to Extremely worried. Participants who selected Prefer not to say were pairwise deleted.
